It was supposed to be a gator farm, but it was just a bunch of sad-looking alligators sitting in muddy water. They had a gift shop that smelled like cheap vanilla candles and dust. I bought a mood ring there, but it always stayed black, which meant I was supposedly stressed all the time. I guess the ring was right, considering how things turned out... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the voices and the special mission stuff. Yeah, once that huge high-energy period ended and I crashed, all of that stopped. I haven't been hearing those things or feeling like I was chosen for anything since I've been feeling empty. Just the exhaustion now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of emptiness and hopelessness are severe enough to interfere with your daily life almost completely?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really does interfere with everything. Like I was saying, after all that energy dropped, I just started feeling empty and hopeless most days. It honestly feels like I've already ruined my future in healthcare before it even really started. Which is really upsetting because I just spent so much money on these new scrubs. They were the expensive kind, the ones with the extra cargo pockets on the leg. You really need those pockets for all the alcohol swabs and pens. I got them in navy blue because that's the mandatory color for support staff at our clinic. Navy blue is such a boring color, honestly. My mom tried to wash them for me before I wore them, but she accidentally left a tissue or a receipt or something in her own pants pocket in the same load, so my brand new dark scrubs came out completely covered in this white fuzz. We had to use a lint roller on them for like forty-five minutes. Peeling the sticky layers off the lint roller is kind of satisfying, though. My cat hates the lint roller, by the way. His name is Cheddar, he's this fat orange tabby, and the second he sees the roller he bolts right under the sofa. He thinks it's the vacuum cleaner. Our vacuum makes this horrible high-pitched squealing noise because the belt is loose, and my dad refuses to replace it. He says he can just fix it with duct tape, but duct tape doesn't fix a rubber belt...\n\nWait, sorry. I lost track again. You were asking if the emptiness stops me from functioning. But yeah, that feeling of just being totally hopeless and having ruined my chances at a career... it's severe enough that I can't really do much of anything right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion most of the time?\n\nPatient: Moving in slow motion? Yeah, definitely slow motion. It's like I'm wading through molasses or something. People at work were actually pointing it out to me. They said I looked really slowed down, and I could totally feel it, too. Like, even just putting a blood pressure cuff away or answering a simple question took me way, way longer than normal. Which is really bad in a clinic, because everything is always go, go, go. You have to turn over those exam rooms so fast. And they use these specific blood pressure cuffs where the Velcro gets all frayed after a few months, and then it doesn't stick right, so you're standing there trying to get a reading while the cuff keeps popping open. It's so embarrassing when the patient is just staring at you. I tried to order new cuffs once, but the medical supply catalog is like a thousand pages long. It's ridiculous. It's thicker than a phone book. Do people even use phone books anymore? I remember my grandma used to use them as booster seats for us at the kitchen table when we were little. Her kitchen always smelled like Pine-Sol and overcooked cabbage. She loved making this weird cabbage soup that had hot dogs cut up in it. I hated it, but she'd make me eat a whole bowl before I could go outside and play. We used to catch lightning bugs in her backyard in these old mayonnaise jars with holes poked in the lid... \n\nBut yeah, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, the slow motion thing. Yeah, I just couldn't move at my normal speed, and everything, even just answering people, took forever.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings of emptiness and hopelessness are getting worse over time or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: They've stayed about the same, honestly. It's just been this constant, heavy feeling. The depressed, guilty, exhausted part has lasted for about nine months now, ever since I crashed after that huge high-energy period. Nine whole months. It feels like an eternity when you're just dragging yourself around. I actually marked it on my calendar when I started feeling really bad. It's one of those big desk calendars with the tear-off pages. I got it at an office supply store because they were having a clearance sale. I love office supply stores. All the different colored pens and the sticky notes... I have a serious weakness for sticky notes. I buy them in every shape. I even have some shaped like little apples. I used to stick them all over my locker in high school to remind myself to do my homework, but the adhesive on the apple ones wasn't very strong, so they'd always curl up and fall off into the bottom of my locker. My locker was a total mess anyway. I shared it with this guy named Kevin who played lacrosse, and his gear always smelled like wet dog and old cheese. I don't know how he stood it. He used to leave his cleats right on top of my textbooks...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? But yeah, to answer your question, the emptiness hasn't really gotten worse or better. It's just been a steady nine months of feeling completely drained and guilty since I crashed.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current feelings of emptiness and hopelessness as mildly bothersome, moderately debilitating, or severely incapacitating?\n\nPatient: Severely incapacitating, definitely. I mean, it got so bad that I couldn't even finish my documentation safely or stay focused on basic patient-support tasks at the clinic. It was a huge mess. Eventually, employee health just pulled me from work completely and sent me for an emergency evaluation. They were actually really nice about it, the nurse in employee health. Her name was Brenda, I think. She had this huge collection of enamel pins on her lanyard. One of them was a little cartoon brain holding a cup of coffee, which I thought was pretty funny. I used to collect pins when I was younger, mostly ones from different national parks my family visited. We went to Yellowstone once and it smelled so strongly of sulfur, like rotten eggs, everywhere you went near the geysers. My sister complained about it the entire trip. She was going through this phase where she only wore black and listened to these really obscure emo bands. She even painted her nails black, but she'd pick at the polish so it always looked chipped and terrible. She used to blast that music from her bedroom, and the bass would rattle the pictures on the hallway wall. One time, a framed photo of my grandparents actually fell off the wall and the glass shattered everywhere. My dad was so mad, he grounded her for two weeks...\n\nOh, wait. Right, the emptiness. But yeah, it's severely incapacitating. I also had to completely stop preparing for my CNA certification because I just couldn't keep up with the studying or anything anymore. So yeah, I can't really function at all."
  }
